🎯 Coaching priority this week

Joel Villatoro — 36.5/85

Houston · weakest section: Save Your Ass (0% of max)

Highest-leverage fix is the Save Your Ass speech, which is missing entirely and is worth more to this specific customer than anything else on the call. He is self-packing a three-bedroom into a 4000 sq ft house, billing at $96 per mover per hour across a five-man crew — $480 an hour — with an estimate of six to seven hours. Every hour of prep he does is $480 off his own bill, and nobody told him. Right after the estimate, in place of 'Any questions there regarding that estimate?', the line should have been: 'Zach, here's how you control that number. Anything you can get boxed, consolidated and stacked in one room before we show up comes straight off the clock — kids' toys, garden tools, anything loose in drawers, photos off the walls. And take a carload yourself every time you drive over; anything small in your car is something we're not carrying down your stairs. Guys who prep well routinely land at the bottom of my range instead of the top. Does that make sense?' Second priority is the differentiator block: No Worries, Meet Your Mover and On-Time Guarantee are all zero, which is 6 of the 20 pricing points, and this customer specifically named a large mirror, a concrete outdoor table, a Peloton and a treadmill — he handed over the damage opening and it went unused. 'That mirror and the concrete table are exactly what our No Worries Damage Coverage is built for — up to $2,000 per move to repair, replace or reimburse accidental damage, included, not an add-on' would have cost ten seconds. Third, and this one is an estimate-accuracy issue, not a script issue: when the customer said the 140-180 box range 'sounds like probably more than I would think,' the answer was 'Okay, cool' and a move to the next topic. That pushback needed to be worked to a number — 'What are you picturing? Let's land on a count, because boxes are the single biggest driver of how long this takes' — because the quote was then set below his own stated typical of seven to eight hours on top of a volume input the customer had just disputed. Two-story origin to two-story destination with workout equipment and a concrete table does not point to the low end. Finally, the walkthrough and the close both ended one step short. He described the virtual walkthrough perfectly and then never booked it, and he offered the email before the schedule, which is what he got. Reverse the order and hold the date: 'October 5th is open right now, and there's no deposit to hold it. Let me lock that in and get you on a ten-minute virtual walkthrough this week so the number in your email is the real number — I'll send the confirmation either way and the key details will be bolded right at the top, so just reply if anything looks off.'

Coaching note: Highest-leverage fix is verification of the two data points this entire call depends on. The only deliverable Arden owes Sue is a link, and both the spelling of her name and her email were left unresolved on the recording — 'R-U-S-S' first, then 'S-U-E-E-D-R-U-F-F,' then 'and then at Gmail @yahoo.com.' The line she needed, once, slowly, at the end: 'Sue, let me read this back to you so I get it exactly right — S-U-E-E-D-R-U-F-F at yahoo dot com, and I'm sending the walkthrough link there right now while we're on the phone. Can you tell me when it lands?' Confirming receipt live turns a hoped-for Monday appointment into a confirmed one. Second priority: she was handed the customer's stated fear and skipped past it. 'Those items are heavy and you have to be careful with them' about a piano and a grandfather clock deserved 'That's exactly what I want to look at on Monday, and it's also what our No Worries Damage Coverage is for — up to $2,000 per move to repair, replace, or reimburse accidental damage. I'm noting the piano and the grandfather clock on your file right now so the crew plans for them.' Practice pairing every stated worry with the differentiator that answers it. Third, use her name — zero uses across seven minutes, all 'ma'am,' with a customer who was clearly warm and told Arden about her late husband. Operational flags to close before Monday: (1) the destination is Presbyterian Village North, a senior-living community — a COI, an elevator or floor, a loading-dock window, and a long interior carry are all live possibilities and none were asked about; a virtual walkthrough of the origin will not reveal any of them, so those questions have to be added to the Monday agenda explicitly. (2) The customer twice emphasized 'a lot of boxes' and 'a lot of books' and was never offered packing services or asked whether the boxes are already packed and sealed — that is both a revenue miss and an estimate-accuracy risk. (3) She said she would be ready earlier if something cancelled; get her on the cancellation list. (4) The 11th was described as the 'latest' availability when it is the earliest — restate the date plainly on Monday so there is no ambiguity about what was held.

✗ Save Your Ass✓ Meet Your Mover✓ On-Time Guarantee✗ Agenda opener✓ Email Handoff
Customer: Unknown  ·  Phone presence: 3 — Adequate
💪 Top strength
The pricing, differentiator, and clock block was a clean 24 out of 24 — the best-executed stretch of script on any call in this batch. He opened it correctly ('let me explain our pricing work. Miss Caitlin is super simple'), gave the rate and the crew in one breath ('an hourly rate of $202 per hour for two movers and we provide a 26 foot box truck'), then ran the full inclusion list without skipping a line: 'gas trucks, taxes, insurance, all the wraps, the pads and bans to protect the item... Even the tools for disassemble and reassembly.' He landed all four no-hidden-fee items, then hit three differentiators back to back — the $2,000 coverage with 'repair, replacement or reimbursement for zero deductible,' Meet Your Mover ('prior to the move, you're gonna receive an email' about who is arriving at her door), and the On-Time Guarantee ('if the movers arrive late, you start to get discount on your final bill') — and closed the clock correctly with on-site start, 2.5-hour minimum, 15-minute increments, and a sense-check: 'Does that all make sense.' His packing explanation was genuinely good teaching, not just a policy read: 'say for example, right now I'm estimating the kitchen and some other minimal items to take around two hours. If the movers will be able to finish the packing in just [an hour] and a half. Then you only pay for [that] plus the packing supplies.' And he handled the one piece of real friction on the call well — when she said 'if they don't figure it out by Saturday, then I can't move on Sunday,' he removed the risk instead of arguing with it: 'you can just give us a call and we can just reschedule you... we don't charge anything extra for rescheduling or if there is any cancellation needed.' That answer is what got him 'Okay, okay, perfect' and the booking.
🎯 Coaching note
Highest-leverage fix: he quoted five to six hours and $1,312 on a bed, three nightstands, a standing desk, a TV and a TV stand, and then never gave her a single way to bring that number down. Right after 'you only pay for the actual time use,' the Save Your Ass speech should have followed: 'Caitlin, here's how you take an hour off that bill. Anything that fits in your car — the loose stuff in drawers, the small kitchen items, the desk cables — take a carload over yourself before Sunday. Get the photos off the walls, and box up whatever you can so the crew is loading, not sorting. Every box you've already packed is time we're not billing you for. Does that make sense?' That is worth ten points on the scorecard and it is worth more than that on estimate accuracy, because the prep speech is what makes a five-to-six-hour quote come in at four. Second priority: reconcile the estimate out loud. Five to six hours at the rate he quoted is $1,010 to $1,212, and he said $1,312 — he needs to say 'that's about $1,212 for the six hours plus roughly $100 in packing supplies,' or the customer does that math herself later and calls it a surprise. Third: close the loop on the email. 'I'm gonna send you a confirmation email' should be 'I'm sending a confirmation email now — the key details are bolded right at the top, your pickup unit 140, drop-off at 3031 Oliver, apartment 1207 on the twelfth floor, Sunday the 6th between 8:30 and 9:00. Read those and reply with any corrections.' On this specific call that matters more than usual, because he mixed up the tenth and twelfth floor mid-call and never confirmed which one is right. Two things to fix in EMMA today: (1) the destination floor needs verifying before the crew is dispatched, and (2) he verbally promised free rescheduling and free cancellation if her lease paperwork slips — that commitment needs to be on the job notes, not just in his memory, along with a note that this booking is contingent on Saturday paperwork.
📞 Phone presence
Consistently courteous with real name use ('Thank you so much for asking Caitlin,' 'keep in mind Caitlin') and he genuinely reassured her on the lease hiccup, but the call was script-forward rather than conversational — he answered his own confirmations ('Got it.' 'You're welcome.') before she spoke, took four dead-air holds while hunting for details, and never turned her 'There's a loading dock' or her paperwork worry into an actual back-and-forth.
🎧 Audio review flagged
The transcript is heavily garbled in exactly the places that matter for grading — the rate reads '$202 per hour,' packing hours read as dollars ('finish the packing in just $1 and a half'), the inventory reads 'Three night and three still standing desk' and 'two autos TV,' and the Meet Your Mover line degrades to 'an email a few year. Movers are helping east tension of changes.' The quoted hourly rate and the Meet Your Mover wording cannot be verified from text, and the clarity/pace bullet cannot be judged fairly without hearing whether the distortion is ASR or delivery.
Items missed (21)
  • No agenda statement. He asked permission — 'is this a good time to talk to you about the move?' — and got an explicit yes, which earns the confirmation bullet, but the customer was never told what was coming or how long it would take. The missing line is the roadmap plus timeframe: 'I'll gather a few details about your move, then walk you through pricing — takes about 10 to 15 minutes.' He did give a mid-call mini-roadmap ('Before I go over pricing structure and estimate, do you have any question...'), which is the right instinct in the wrong place.
  • Save Your Ass section never happened — 0/10, and this is the most expensive miss on the call. No 'prep before move day means a shorter move and a lower bill,' no consolidation examples (kids' toys, garden tools, loose items in drawers, photos off walls), no 'take a carload' tip. The closest he came was the estimate caveat 'you only pay for the actual time use... if the move goes faster than what we have anticipated, then of course you pay less' — which tells her the meter is honest but never tells her how to move it. On a job he himself estimated at five to six hours with partial packing, the prep speech was the single highest-value thing he could have said.
  • Estimate does not reconcile with the price quoted. He said 'I am estimating the move to take around five to six hours' and then 'it's good to budget an overall of $1,312.' At the $202/hr two-mover rate he had just quoted, five to six hours is $1,010 to $1,212 — $1,312 sits above the top of his own stated range and he never broke out what the extra covers (packing supplies, presumably). The customer was handed a range and a number that don't match and no bridge between them.
  • Estimate is likely conservative for the load described. The inventory he read back was 'a bed. Three night[stands] and... standing desk... two autos TV and a TV stand' — no appliances, no heavy or large items, nothing fragile, everything inside a first-floor apartment. He anchored on 'a one bedroom apartment usually takes two movers between two and a half to three and a half hours' and then landed at five to six for a load materially lighter than a full one-bedroom. Elevator access and two hours of kitchen packing justify some of the lift; the whole 2.5-hour add is not visibly justified. This is the exact overestimate-on-a-light-job pattern that shows up in the estimate-accuracy work.
  • Drive time between origin and destination was never mentioned as an estimate variable, and the total drive was never discussed with the customer at all.
  • Walk distance ignored — and the customer volunteered the opening. She said 'There's a loading dock' and he moved straight past it to pricing. Loading dock to apartment 1207 on the twelfth floor is a real time driver on this job and it was never sized up or explained as a variable.
  • No equivalents or word pictures used anywhere (no school-bus reference or similar) to make the truck size or the time estimate concrete.
  • Disassembly and reassembly time never factored into the estimate. He mentioned the tools are included and that there are no assembly fees, but a bed and a standing desk both have to come apart and go back together, and that time was never named as part of why the job runs five to six hours.
  • No zip codes captured or confirmed on either end, and the origin address was never read back — only 'Unit number 140.' The destination came out as '3031 Oliver Street, apartment 1207.' On a call whose first move was confirming the online form, both addresses should have been verified out loud.
  • Floor confusion on the destination, self-corrected mid-sentence but never cleanly confirmed: 'This is on the tenth floor. Can we reserved the elevator by any chance? Oh, is this on the twelfth floor?' He then used 'twelve floor' in the estimate. Twelfth is presumably right, but it was never restated back for confirmation.
  • Box count never asked. He explained that supplies are billed on top — 'you just have to pay for the additional packing supply cost for the boxes. The tapes... wraps' — without ever asking how many boxes she has already packed or how many the kitchen pack will need, so the supply cost inside that $1,312 is unquantified for her.
  • Special considerations never probed — no question about long walks, narrow hallways, or tight turns. His probing block covered heavy or large items, appliances, and fragile items (all specialty-item questions), not access geometry.
  • Additional stops never asked. Single destination is likely, but it was assumed rather than confirmed.
  • No moving-stressor question — 'is there anything you're particularly worried about with this move?' was never asked. She had one, and she raised it herself at the booking moment: 'my new lease should be ready to go for Saturday. But there has been a little bit of a hiccup and just like admin paperwork on their side.' A stressor question in the probe would have surfaced that five minutes earlier, before he anchored her on a date.
  • No BOLDED-details framing for the confirmation email and no 'reply with any corrections.' He said only 'I'm gonna send you a confirmation email' — the verification step that catches wrong addresses and wrong floors was skipped, on a call that had a floor mix-up in it.
  • No 'any other questions before I let you go?' at the close. He substituted a callback invite — 'If there's any question clarification please feel free to give us a callback' — and then closed himself out, 'Thank you ma'am. You're welcome. Bye for now.'
  • Business hours and how to reach us never given. He told her twice to 'give us a call' if the lease paperwork slips, without ever saying when someone would be there to answer or what number to use.
  • 'No deposit' never said in those words. 'You only pay after the actual move is done' carries the substance, so the bullet is credited, but the brand differentiator went unnamed.
  • No Worries Damage Coverage delivered by substance but not by name — transcribed as 'up to $2,000 fee evaluation coverage.' The 60-cent-per-pound baseline was never mentioned, so she has no reference point for what makes the $2,000 coverage worth anything.
  • No rapport moment anywhere in seven minutes. The customer's entire contribution was 'Yes,' 'No,' 'Okay,' and 'Yup' until she raised the lease problem — the one time she spoke in full sentences.
  • Four separate dead-air holds in a seven-minute call ('Okay, one second,' 'Okay, 11 second,' 'give me one second,' 'I'm gonna go ahead and one second') suggest he was hunting in EMMA while she waited, twice in the middle of his own sentence.

✓ Save Your Ass✗ Meet Your Mover✓ On-Time Guarantee✗ Agenda opener✓ Email Handoff
Customer: Unknown  ·  Phone presence: 4 — Engaging
💪 Top strength
The probe and the pricing block were near-textbook, and the recovery was better. He inherited an annoyed customer — six minutes on hold, then a voicemail prompt — and turned it around inside two exchanges by owning it plainly ('I'm sorry that you got placed on hold. I had left the office to go pick up a truck from the shop') and then riffing on her own joke about the hold music ('I'm glad that at least it was somewhat pleasant six minutes'). From there the discovery was genuinely thorough: origin and destination addresses with zips, square footage at both ends, room-by-room inventory including the partial-bedroom nuance ('So we really aren't moving any bedroom furniture at all'), garage and backyard, appliances, gun safes and workout equipment, fragile items, parking and walk distance at BOTH locations, third floor plus elevator, and whether the elevator can be reserved. He caught the access wrinkle she raised and logged it out loud: 'I'll just put access might be a little tricky for us.' The pricing section hit almost every bullet verbatim — 'pricing is super simple on Wednesdays and Thursdays. We charge $97 per hour per mover... So that'd be $194 an hour. Everything is included in that hourly rate? So the truck gas taxes insurance, all of the pads wrap and bands... and then any tools needed for disassemble or reassembly' — followed by a clean no-hidden-fees pitch, the full clock explanation (starts on arrival with the walkthrough, 2.5-hour minimum, 15-minute prorated increments), a sense-check, the On-Time Guarantee, and no-deposit plus no cancellation fee. His handling of her one real objection was the best moment on the call: instead of a generic reassurance about extra boxes he gave her the capacity equivalent and a plan — 'our box trucks they're 26 footers. They can hold the content of about a fully furnished seventeen to 1800 square foot home... If you start creeping into maybe triple digits give me a call let me know and we can allocate maybe a third person.' He also framed budget honestly and repeatedly: 'if it ends up taking less time than what I've quoted you, it'll end up being cheaper for you at the end of the day.'
🎯 Coaching note
Highest-leverage fix: he built the whole sale and then walked away from the close. She had already said yes to the 30th and the 1st. The moment she said 'I've got three quotes right now and let me stop there,' the line was: 'Totally fair, Jinny — and here's the easy part. There's no deposit and no cancellation fee, so let me put the 30th and the 1st on the schedule right now while they're open. If one of the other quotes wins, you call me and I take you off, no charge. That way you're not comparison shopping against a calendar that's filling up.' That is a zero-risk ask he had already earned, and he had every ingredient in hand — he even delivered the no-deposit and no-cancellation-fee lines thirty seconds later, just as a parting reassurance instead of as the close. Second priority: if he isn't going to book it, book the follow-up. He ended on 'just call us back at this number here' and put the next move entirely on an 81-year-old customer holding three bids. He should have said 'I'll give you a call Tuesday to see where you landed — does late morning work?' Third: No Worries Damage Coverage is missing from a call where she inventoried artwork, glass cabinet doors, and a wicker swing chair. When she said 'I think the artwork is pretty. I think it's safe in a box,' the answer was 'Good — and either way you're covered. Our No Worries Damage Coverage covers up to $2,000 per move to repair, replace, or reimburse anything we accidentally damage, and it's included, not an add-on.' Pair that with Meet Your Mover, which is a two-second add and disproportionately reassuring for a solo senior customer hosting a crew two days running. Fourth, and this one is Amanda's rock, not just Grant's: the estimate math. 7.5 hours priced at the very top of his own stated 'seven to eight hours' range for a FULL 1200 sqft home, on a move with no bedroom furniture, no coffee table, and no TV, is padding — and he said the quiet part out loud: 'since we don't know how long the walk is at the unload location I'm gonna play it safe with the estimate and just kind of air on the side of caution.' The disclosure is honest and the hourly model protects her, but a $3,185 number going up against two other bids can lose a job it should have won. The better move is to quote the realistic middle (6.5 on the move day given the reduced inventory), name the unload walk as the one variable, and tell her exactly what would push it up. Last, small but real: fix the opener so the customer catches his name. Nineteen minutes in she asked 'what was your name again? Frank.' A clean 'Hi Jinny, this is Grant with Einstein Moving Company' plus the agenda statement up front fixes both the name problem and the missing roadmap in one sentence.
📞 Phone presence
He converted an irritated customer into a chatty one through real rapport — owning the six-minute hold, joking about the smoker ('No, like, 500 gallon smokers or anything like that. Nothing crazy back there'), following her Lockheed tangent ('I have a friend that works with them right now'), heavy natural name use, and a warm close she reciprocated with 'Thank you so much' — held back from a 5 only by never asking how she was doing and by her not retaining his name.
Items missed (13)
  • No upfront agenda statement and no timeframe. The roadmap came at minute ~10, after the entire probe: 'let me walk you through how our pricing is gonna work and then I'll go over your estimate specifically for you.' Good language, wrong place — the customer sat through a long inventory interview with no idea how long it would take or what was coming. No '10-15 minutes' framing anywhere.
  • Never asked 'how are you today?' The customer opened by telling him she had been on hold six minutes and got dumped to voicemail. He apologized ('I'm sorry that you got placed on hold... I had left the office to go pick up a truck from the shop') but never turned it into a check-in on her.
  • Never asked the moving-stressor question. This customer handed him the answer unprompted — she volunteered that she is 81 ('I'm not probably gonna be doing a whole lot of packing') and that a previous mover refused her extra boxes on move day ('they wouldn't take them. I had to call somebody at the last minute'). He answered the box fear well but never asked 'is there anything you're particularly worried about with this move?', so he found the stressor by luck rather than by process.
  • No Worries Damage Coverage never mentioned. 'Insurance' appeared only as a line item inside the hourly rate — 'the truck gas taxes insurance' — with zero explanation of the $2,000 per-move repair/replace/reimburse coverage. This on a call where the customer walked him through artwork, a glass-door TV console, a wicker swing chair on a metal stand, and a drop-leaf table, and said 'I think it's safe in a box.'
  • Meet Your Mover never mentioned. No reference to the pre-move email with the mover's photo — a natural fit for an 81-year-old customer letting a two-man crew into her house for two consecutive days.
  • No 60c/lb baseline. The full insurance explanation (No Worries plus the legal baseline) never happened, so the customer has no idea what coverage she actually has.
  • No consolidation examples and no 'take a carload' tip. The Save Your Ass content only appeared reactively, when she asked 'if I have boxes packed they can go to, right?' and he answered 'whatever you pack before we get there, we'll just make this cheaper and quicker for you.' True, but nothing about loose items in drawers, photos off walls, garden tools, or small stuff in the car — and she is actively deciding what to take, which is exactly when those tips land.
  • No booking attempt. This is the biggest miss on the call. He got a yes on the two-day structure ('we could come out on maybe Wednesday the thirtieth to pack... and then on Thursday the first to actually move it. Is that something you'd be okay with?' / 'Yes.') and then never asked for the business. When she said 'I've got a couple of quotes so I'll just review them and... I think I've got three quotes right now,' he answered 'Of course, I totally understand' and pivoted to no-deposit. No 'want me to hold the 30th and 1st for you?', no follow-up date set, no next-step commitment on a comparison-shopping customer who had already agreed to the dates.
  • No BOLDED-details framing for the confirmation email and no 'reply with any corrections.' He said 'answer the questions that are at the bottom of the quote' — that is a booking instruction, not an ask to verify the details, which matters here because the inventory was unusually complicated (partial bedrooms, no bedroom furniture, no coffee table, no TV).
  • Hours and how to reach us not given. Only 'just call us back at this number here.' No office hours.
  • Drive time never explained as a time variable. The customer volunteered 'somewhere between seven and eight miles' and he never told her the drive counts on the clock, or whether Arlington sits inside the Fort Worth service zone (no trip fee was quoted either way).
  • Estimate delivery was internally inconsistent and reads padded. He said 'about six and a half maybe seven and a half hours on each day on the first day' and then 'both days, about seven and a half hours' in the same breath, and priced off 7.5. 🎯 Coaching note
Highest-leverage fix: Garrison built the case for No Worries Damage Coverage and then never delivered it. He told this customer to go shop other movers on exactly our strongest differentiator — 'if your mover ends up breaking it, damaging it, scuff it, scratching it... make sure you're asking about that, asking about their coverage' — without ever stating ours. The line he needed, right there, was: 'And here's ours, Zen — No Worries Damage Coverage, up to $2,000 per move to repair, replace, or reimburse any accidental damage, included in that hourly rate at no extra cost. Most movers will only cover 60 cents a pound, which on a brand-new nine-foot closet system means they hand you about twenty dollars for a cabinet you just paid hundreds for. That's the gap I'd want you comparing.' That single paragraph reframes $595 from 'fifty dollars over my highest quote' into insurance on a new purchase, and it is the only argument on this call that could have beaten the price objection. Second priority, and it is nearly as costly: there was no booking attempt. She said 'I'm trying to get this done today, so I can find a mover and get in here tomorrow' and he had already confirmed tomorrow was open — that is a buying signal plus a confirmed date, and the call still ended on 'I'll shoot that email over to you.' Pair the coverage pitch with the no-deposit close: 'There's no deposit and nothing due today, so let me hold tomorrow morning for you while you finish comparing — if you find something better, you cancel and it costs you nothing.' A price shopper who has to choose today will almost always take a free hold. Third, the clock section is where the objection was actually born, and he narrated the minimum without a sense-check. Say the 2.5-hour minimum, then the 15-minute prorated increments after it, then 'Does that make sense?' and let her react before you name the total. Business risk to fix in EMMA today: the contact data on this lead is not trustworthy, and the entire follow-up plan rests on it. The email came through two different ways in one breath — 'Zen. Lo @yahoo.com Z-E-N-L-O-P-E-R @yahoo.com' — and he never read it back. The callback-number confirmation came back as 'this is Marshall,' which was never resolved. He also never captured the origin zip on 2120 Denton Drive. Verify the address, the number, and the zip before that comparison email goes out, because if it bounces this lead is simply gone. 🎯 Coaching note
Highest-leverage fix: ask for the booking. Danah did almost everything needed to earn this move and then let it walk. She personally confirmed September 19 was open in the first two minutes, ran a thorough 20-minute intake, explained No Worries better than most of the team, and told the customer 'we don't require deposit to book' — and then closed with 'if you wanna go ahead and move forward, just let us know.' Second priority, and this one is a company-level issue, not just a Danah issue: she let the customer set the estimate. Her own model said 2.5-4 hours. The customer asked to inflate it because a third party is reimbursing, and Danah said 'we'll over estimate it a little bit just to be on the safer side,' floated 4.5 hours out loud, then wrote 3.5-4.5. That is a padded number entering EMMA for a stated reason that has nothing to do with the job — and it is the exact behavior that keeps estimate accuracy stuck in the 40s. Worth flagging for the estimate-accuracy work: this is a live example of the pressure that inflates apartment and small-move estimates, coming from the customer side rather than the manager side. Third: the prep speech. The customer said she is boxing next week and asked what size boxes to use — a wide-open door for both Save Your Ass and an Ecobox referral, and neither came through. Every trip up and down those stairs the crew doesn't have to make is time off your clock. Does that make sense?' Second priority: three of the five differentiators went unspoken on a call that was begging for all three. A customer moving a 250 lb sauna, a squat rack and two refrigerators needed 'That's exactly what our No Worries Damage Coverage is for — up to $2,000 per move to repair, replace or reimburse accidental damage'; a customer who is a first-time Einstein buyer needed Meet Your Mover; and a Saturday two-truck job needed the On-Time Guarantee. Third: the Save Your Ass speech was left on the table after the customer volunteered that she'd be fully packed. Does that make sense?' Business risks to close today: (1) The final commitment is genuinely unclear — 'this estimate includes moving the sign-up. But should be disassembled in advance and we won't handle the a' — Patricia has been told the sauna is in the quote, must be pre-disassembled by her, and cannot be disassembled by us, and she is now deciding with her husband whether that's a deal breaker. That needs one unambiguous written sentence in the email, or this job is lost or disputed. (2) Patricia asked 'What if we don't want to take it apart, is there an option?' and never got an answer — the honest answer, including whether we can refer a sauna installer, is what wins this move. (3) Amanda's policy check: an 1850 sqft home with a garage gym, sauna and patio furniture was quoted over the phone rather than routed to a virtual walkthrough, and Amy priced two trucks and four movers off a text photo and a customer's own square footage. This is the exact profile the walkthrough gate exists for, and it is also an estimate-accuracy exposure on a $2,664–$3,108 job.

Lukas ran a fast, confident, well-organized outbound callback and gave a price-shopping customer exactly what she called for, which she said out loud twice: 'that is exactly what I needed to know. He then closed better than most: he volunteered no deposit and no reschedule fee before being asked ('we don't require any deposit to get you on the schedule as well just to kind of put that on the radar'), answered her holiday-hours question with real specifics ('We're open Monday, we're even open tomorrow if need be'), made a direct and well-framed booking attempt that respected where she actually was in her process ('would you like to secure the spot while you're looking around... just in case, or just wanna get the quote hold onto it and kind of compare quotes'), and verified her email address letter by letter before promising delivery in minutes. Budget framing was consistent and on-brand throughout: 'you do only pay for the time that gets used' and 'if it takes less time... by all means, we'll just pay less at the end of the day.'
🎯 Coaching note
Highest-leverage fix is the probe, because on this call the probe is the estimate. Lukas quoted 6.5 hours and $2,374 without ever asking whether the daughter's apartment is on the second floor, how far the walk to the truck is, how many boxes there are, or whether the glass-top desk needs breaking down — and he quoted it to a customer who had already told him she uses a wheelchair and that the desk 'comes apart.' Before the price he needed to say: 'Delia, two quick things so I get your hours right — is her apartment upstairs or ground floor, and how far is the walk from her door to where the truck can park? And you mentioned the glass-top desk comes apart — we'll break that down and rebuild it at the house, that's included, I just want to build the time in.' Those three answers are the difference between a quote that holds and a bad move-day surprise on a job that is already an estimate-accuracy risk. Second priority: she asked for packing and he never answered. 'Let me see if I can find out if there are people who would pack up our stuff' is a buying signal for a higher-ticket service, and the line was 'We absolutely pack — it's the same $96 per mover per hour plus materials, and for a load this size it usually adds an hour or two. Want me to quote it both ways so you can see the difference?' That is revenue left on the table and an accuracy gap at the same time. Third: three differentiators went unsaid on the call where they mattered most. No Worries in particular was gift-wrapped — glass furniture and computers going 200 miles down the highway to a customer comparing bids on price alone. 'Everything gets wrapped and padded, and you're covered by our No Worries Damage Coverage — up to $2,000 per move to repair, replace, or reimburse anything we damage. That's included, not an add-on.' Pair that with Meet Your Mover and the On-Time Guarantee and she has three reasons to pick Einstein that no competitor's cheaper hourly rate answers. Fourth, and this is the human one: she told him she is 69, her husband is nearly 75, she has a bad back, and her daughter is in a wheelchair after a hard living situation. Every disclosure got 'For sure' or 'Sweet.' One sentence would have changed the whole call — 'That sounds like a lot to carry on your own, and honestly that's exactly what we're for — you don't need to lift a thing, our guys will handle all of it.' She is buying relief, not hours. Finally, slow the pricing recap down and separate it into pieces with a check after each: rate, then travel charge, then hours, then total, then 'does that all line up with what you were expecting?' Stacking five numbers into one self-corrected sentence is how a price-shopper writes down the wrong figure and compares it against a competitor.
📞 Phone presence
Upbeat and easy to talk to on the surface, and the customer left the call happy ('Thank you so much'), but the warmth was filler rather than connection — 'Sweet,' 'For sure' and 'by all means' repeated throughout while significant personal disclosures about her age, her back, and her daughter's wheelchair drew no empathy at all, and her name was used once and mispronounced.

💪 Top strength

Dustin caught the trap in his own system: the quote form said four-bedroom, but the customer explained she was 'going from 1800 and, and fifty square feet down to like, 539,' and instead of quoting the form Dustin re-priced the actual load out loud, on the record: 'This move is a little other than typical, obviously, she's moving out of a four bedroom home that usually would take three to four guys. Six to eight hours. In this case I think the appropriate team size is a two person team.' That is exactly the manual override the estimate-accuracy rock is trying to produce. He then built the estimate from real variables rather than assumptions — he qualified the piano ('it's just a basic up, right. It's not like a grand or baby grand'), confirmed it was going to a ground floor, captured the third address, marked the stop ('let me mark drop-off piano there'), and validated both drive legs with the customer ('does it sound right that it's about twenty to 25 minutes from the Oana drive address?'). Two probes stand out. First, the box count, where he gave the customer an anchor and his reasoning instead of an open question: 'normally... moving into an apartment that size would expect to move around 25 boxes. Do you think she'll be taken about 25 boxes with her?' — then explained the range and pre-absorbed the downside, 'obviously if it's less, that's definitely not an issue at all.' Second, the walk-distance probe, which almost nobody runs: 'sometimes it's a little bit of a longer than typical traverse from the truck up to the unit... Do you have a guess of how far her unit is from the loading dock?' When the customer didn't know, Dustin bounded it with an equivalent rather than dropping it, and the customer volunteered the answer he needed ('once you hit the second floor, she's not very far'). He also handled scheduling with real transparency — weekend lead times, discounted versus standard days, the honest 'if it were a Friday or a Saturday that would slightly increase that hourly rate' — and closed cleanly on the scarcity he had established earlier: 'you wanted to go ahead and get that locked in on that last truck we have.' The customer's own verdict is the proof: 'This was a lot easier than I thought it was gonna be.'
🎯 Coaching note
Highest-leverage fix by a wide margin: Dustin has no Save Your Ass speech. Zero of ten, and the customer handed him the opening unprompted when he said 'I'm gonna be taking some stuff over to and so it'll probably be less than that.' The reply needed to be: 'Kenneth, that actually helps you twice — since this is hourly, every carload you take over yourself is time our guys don't spend, so the more you can consolidate before we get there the shorter the bill. Biggest wins are the loose stuff: empty out the dresser drawers into boxes, get the photos and frames off the walls, and box up anything small enough to hand-carry. Anything that fits in your car, take it. Does that make sense?' On a job priced at $192/hour where he is already forecasting the move runs past the minimum, that speech is the difference between a 3.5-hour move and a 4.5-hour one, and it is the same lever the estimate-accuracy rock is chasing from the other direction. Second priority: the three differentiators were all skipped on the call that needed them most. This is an elderly woman moving into assisted living, coordinated by her son, with a piano going to a third party — Meet Your Mover, No Worries, and the On-Time Guarantee are the whole reassurance package for a remote decision-maker, and none of them were spoken. Bundle them where the pricing was already landing: 'That rate includes our No Worries Damage Coverage — up to $2,000 per move to repair, replace, or reimburse any accidental damage, and I'm noting the piano on the job so the crew brings the board and straps. A few days before the move you'll get a Meet Your Mover email with a photo and a little background on the guys coming, so nobody's a stranger at the door. And we back the arrival window with an On-Time Guarantee — if we miss it, we start discounting immediately.' Third, and this one is a real business risk, not just a scorecard miss: Dustin quoted a floor of 2.5 hours on a three-address, two-mover, piano-plus-25-boxes job with 35-40 minutes of driving and a 30-yard elevator carry, and then reinforced the anchor with 'I do think you're gonna land somewhere in between those two numbers.' That range should have opened at 3.5, and the piano probably wants three movers rather than two, which also protects the crew. Quoting a floor nobody can hit is how a 52% estimate-accuracy number gets made, and it converts a delighted customer into a surprised invoice. Two smaller cleanups: say the customer's name — he had 'Kenneth' from second one and never used it, and the customer signed off calling him 'Justin,' which is what a fifteen-minute call with no names in it sounds like. And add the two missing close lines: 'There's no deposit — nothing to pay to hold the date,' and 'The top of that email has the key details in bold; give it a quick read and reply if anything's off.' That second one matters more than usual here, because Dustin entered placeholder data on the piano address ('I'm gonna put a thousand square feet... I'm gonna call it a three bedroom house') and estimated the destination walk distance himself, so the confirmation email is the only checkpoint where the customer can catch a wrong assumption before move day.

🎯 Coaching note
Highest-leverage fix: Jack talked about liability for four straight sentences and never once said what Einstein covers. Right after 'we normally just need a waiver that says we are not liable once this leaves,' the line he needed was: 'And to be clear on the other side of that — everything we carry on our truck is protected by our No Worries Damage Coverage, up to $2,000 per move to repair, replace, or reimburse accidental damage. The waiver only applies to what goes on your U-Haul, because we can't control how that gets driven.' As it stands, the only insurance content this customer heard was Einstein disclaiming responsibility. Rule to drill: never end a liability sentence on the exclusion — always land on the coverage. Second priority is the Save Your Ass speech, which is worth real money on this specific job. Jack told her she might beat his estimate and then gave her nothing to act on. He should have said: 'Ana, since this is all hourly, here's how you shave time off the bill — have the U-Haul parked, unlocked and empty when we get there, have your bungee straps out and the waiver signed, strip the bedding off both mattresses, and clear a path from each bedroom to the door. She said twice that a mattress is going upstairs and he never acknowledged it or built it into the estimate. Every quote needs 'is anything going up or down stairs at either place?' before a number leaves his mouth, because on a slay bed that answer can move the estimate by an hour. Two business risks to close in EMMA today: (1) Jack verbally agreed to load a third-party U-Haul contingent on a signed liability waiver and the customer supplying her own straps — none of that is confirmed as logged on the job, and if the waiver isn't in hand on Wednesday the crew is either refusing work the branch manager already promised or loading uninsured. (2) He agreed to open-ended extra furniture rearranging at the Spire Haven address on an hourly job with no scope or clock caveat; the job notes should say the add-on work is billable time so nobody is surprised at the invoice. He diagnosed it correctly and then never told her where to send things. Repeat customers should always leave the call with a direct number and the branch's hours.

Note: this is a high-value call type that the current 85-pt scorecard does not cover — see coaching note.
Coaching note: Highest-leverage fix: when a customer tells you the move might not happen, close the loop before you hang up. Risha said the elevators are broken and she might postpone, and the entire response was 'I will make that note in here for you' — then the call ran another four minutes on inventory and ended with 'have a good weekend.' What she needed to say: 'Risha, let's plan for both so you're not guessing. I'm noting the elevator and I'll flag the crew. If it does end up being a five-flight carry on a one-bedroom, that's roughly two extra hours, so figure closer to $X than the estimate you have — I'd rather tell you that now than on move day. Can you check with your building Friday and reply to the confirmation email either way by Saturday? And if you need to push it, tell me now and I'll hold you a day the following week so you're not starting over.' That single exchange protects the revenue, protects the estimate, and gives the customer the numbers she is currently making the decision without. Second priority: this call sat four days out from move day with a fifth-floor carry in play, which is the highest-value Save Your Ass moment there is, and it was skipped entirely — 'anything small that can go in your car, take a carload; get the loose stuff out of drawers and the photos off the walls, and you'll knock real time off the bill, especially if it's stairs.' Third, and cheap to fix: confirm the name in the first ten seconds. She said 'Theresa,' the account says Risha, the rep said 'Aisha,' and the name was never used again — on a warm call to a booked customer that is free rapport left on the table. Process flag for Amanda, not for Chauny: this is the second Chauny call in the sample set to land as a skip, and both times the reason was that the 85-pt scorecard has no coverage for non-quoting calls (8/25 was a cancellation save, this one is a pre-move confirmation). Confirmation calls are where estimate accuracy is actually won or lost — this call had two elevator variables and a stair contingency that should have re-scoped the job and didn't — so a short confirmation-call mini-rubric (verify details on file, re-scope any changed variable with a revised range, Save Your Ass, Meet Your Mover, set a check-in on any open risk) would put real coaching signal on a call type currently scored as nothing.

💪 Top strength
Rance's technical estimating and objection handling were genuinely strong and are the reason this call scored where it did. He built the estimate transparently from a baseline and adjusted it with a real, teachable rule of thumb: 'Generally a two bedroom apartment move is gonna take two movers about three and a half to five and a half hours to complete however, in your case because we're going from the thirty second to the eighth floor. Those elevators do add quite a bit of time... it's about thirty minutes for every five floors is what we typically experience an elevator ads to the move.' When the customer pushed back that it was nearly double the time, Rance didn't retreat or hedge — he re-explained the mechanism (transport time, elevator capacity, reserved-elevator reality) and landed the same number. He also framed his own conservatism honestly rather than hiding it: 'I'm kind of, you know, being conservative with this estimate... I'd kind of rather, you know, give you good news at the end of the move then unexpected bad news.' That is exactly the estimate-accuracy posture the team is trying to build. His specialty-item probe was thorough and specific ('anything unusually, like, large bulky heavy, like a sleeper sofa. Like a large treadmill, an upright piano, gun safe?' then a second pass on fragile items that surfaced the marble coffee table and glass side table). We don't use any daily contract Ted labors' — which landed well enough that the customer summarized the value prop back to him correctly and unprompted.
🎯 Coaching note

He handled a 30% price objection well, the customer explicitly said 'I really wanted to do this with you guys,' and Rance let him hang up to go call competitors without ever attempting a booking or mentioning that it costs nothing to hold the date. (3) An 8-9 hour two-mover job is a long day with real fatigue and window risk; Rance correctly noted three movers lands 'about the same' price in 6-7 hours, and that should be the recommended configuration in EMMA, not the fallback. Finally, the basics cost him 15 points that require no skill at all: get and use the customer's name, return the 'how are you,' set the agenda with a 'sound good?', explain when the clock starts, and ask 'any other questions before I let you go?' Ending a price conversation with a real dollar total instead of an hourly rate is exactly right and most reps do not do it.
🎯 Coaching note
Highest-leverage fix: Chris had a loyal repeat customer, on the phone, asking to be sold, with a small well-understood job at two addresses he already had — and he handed the close to a mover who was off work that day. Her only hesitation was whether the job clears the 2.5-hour minimum, and she told him the answer to that ('there's not much left in there. She handed him that comparison herself ('I've had other moving companies destroy my stuff and he can't get a hold of them'). Turning her own words into the differentiator is free and he walked past it. Third, use her name — zero uses in a 13-minute call with a repeat customer who opened by introducing herself. Two business items for Amanda, separate from the score: (1) Chris verbally offered to waive the 2.5-hour minimum — 'we could lower the minimum in your case' — which is a pricing exception given on the fly with no dollar figure, no approval trail, and nothing in writing, and it is now something this customer expects; worth confirming whether BMs have that latitude. (2) There are two open service issues on this account with no visible ownership: the chipped table from the 9/3 move, and the missing glass top from the broken-into unit that she believes Einstein was going to help locate ('we cause you were going to come and move this stuff around and find the glass').
